Alright, so I need to find the equation of a parabola given its vertex at (0, 0) and focus at (0, 4). Hmm, okay. Let me think about how parabolas work.

First off, a parabola is the set of all points that are equidistant from a fixed point called the focus and a fixed line called the directrix. Since the vertex is halfway between the focus and the directrix, I can figure out where the directrix is.

The vertex here is at (0, 0), and the focus is at (0, 4). That means the distance from the vertex to the focus is 4 units upwards along the y-axis. Therefore, the directrix should be 4 units below the vertex on the same axis, which would place it at y = -4.

Now, using the standard form of a vertical parabola with vertex at (h, k), the equation is usually given by \( y = \frac{1}{4p}(x - h)^2 + k \) where p is the distance from the vertex to the focus. In this case, since the focus is 4 units above the vertex, p would be 4.

Plugging in the values, h and k are both 0 because the vertex is at (0, 0). So plugging into the formula: \( y = \frac{1}{4*4}x^2 + 0 \), which simplifies to \( y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \).

Wait a second, let me make sure. If p is positive when the parabola opens upwards and negative otherwise. Here, since it's opening upward because the focus is above the vertex, so yes, p=4 is correct.

So simplifying \( y = (1/16)x^2 \) should be the right equation.

The equation of the parabola with vertex at (0, 0) and focus at (0, 4) is derived as follows:

Since the focus is above the vertex, the parabola opens upward. The standard form for such a parabola is:
\[ y = \frac{1}{4p}x^2 \]
where \( p \) is the distance from the vertex to the focus.

Here, \( p = 4 \), so substituting into the equation:
\[ y = \frac{1}{16}x^2 \]

Thus, the final equation of the parabola is:

To solve the equation \( S = 85 + \frac{1}{4}S \), we start by isolating \( S \). 

First, subtract \(\frac{1}{4}S\) from both sides:
\[
S - \frac{1}{4}S = 85
\]

This simplifies to:
\[
\frac{3}{4}S = 85
\]

Next, solve for \( S \) by multiplying both sides by the reciprocal of \(\frac{3}{4}\), which is \(\frac{4}{3}\):
\[
S = 85 \times \frac{4}{3}
\]

Calculate the result:
\[
S = \frac{340}{3}
\]

Thus, the solution is \boxed{\dfrac{340}{3}}.

Alright, let's tackle this problem step by step. We have the equation of a parabola given by \( y^2 = 32x \). Our goal is to find its focus and directrix.

First, I recall that the standard form of a horizontally opening parabola is \( y^2 = 4px \), where:
- The vertex is at the origin (0,0).
- The focus is located at \( (p, 0) \).
- The directrix is the vertical line \( x = -p \).

Comparing this standard form with our given equation \( y^2 = 32x \), I can see that:
\[ 4p = 32 \]

To find \( p \), I'll solve for it:
\[ p = \frac{32}{4} = 8 \]

Now, substituting \( p = 8 \) back into the focus and directrix formulas:
- **Focus**: \( (8, 0) \)
- **Directrix**: \( x = -8 \)

These are the required properties of the parabola.

Okay, I need to find the equation of a parabola given that it has focus at (2, 3) and directrix x = -1. Alright, let's start by recalling some basic properties of parabolas.

First, a parabola is defined as the set of all points where each point is equidistant from the focus and the directrix. That means for any point (x, y) on the parabola, the distance to the focus (2, 3) should be equal to its perpendicular distance to the directrix x = -1.

Since the directrix is a vertical line, the distance from any point (x, y) to the directrix is simply |x - (-1)|, which simplifies to |x + 1|. So that's straightforward.

Now, for the focus at (2, 3), the distance from (x, y) to this point can be calculated using the distance formula. The distance squared would be (x - 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2. Since we're equating distances, I'll set up an equation where these two distances are equal.

So setting them equal: sqrt[(x - 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2] = |x + 1|. To eliminate the square root and absolute value, I can square both sides of the equation. That gives me [(x - 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2]^2 = (x + 1)^2.

Wait a minute, that doesn't seem right because squaring the left side would actually result in [(x - 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2]^2, which is more complicated than I expected. Maybe there's a better way to approach this without squaring both sides directly.

Alternatively, since we're dealing with squares on one side and linear terms on the other, perhaps it would be better to move all terms to one side first before expanding. Let me write out the equation again:

sqrt[(x - 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2] = |x + 1|

Squaring both sides without absolute value squared should suffice because (|a|)^2 is a^2 regardless of whether a is positive or negative.

So, squaring both sides gives: (x - 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = (x + 1)^2.

Now, I'll expand the left side:

(x^2 - 4x + 4) + (y^2 - 6y + 9) = x^2 + 2x + 1

Combining like terms on the left: x^2 - 4x + 4 + y^2 - 6y + 9.

So that's x^2 + y^2 - 4x - 6y + 13.

Setting equal to the right side, which is x^2 + 2x + 1.

Now, subtract (x^2 + 2x + 1) from both sides:

(x^2 + y^2 - 4x - 6y + 13) - (x^2 + 2x + 1) = 0

Simplifying: x^2 - x^2 cancels out, leaving y^2 - 6y + (-4x - 2x) becomes -6x, and then constants: 13 -1 is 12.

So, the equation becomes y^2 - 6y - 6x + 12 = 0.

Hmm, I think that's as simplified as it gets unless we need to rearrange terms or complete the square for y. Let me try completing the square for y:

To find the vertex form of the quadratic equation \( y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \), we'll complete the square.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \) from the first two terms:**
   \[
   y = 3(x^2 + 2x) + 5
   \]

2. **Identify the coefficient of \( x \), which is 2, take half of it to get 1, and square it to get 1² = 1.**

3. **Add and subtract this squared value inside the parentheses:**
   \[
   y = 3\left(x^2 + 2x + 1 - 1\right) + 5
   \]

4. **Rewrite as a perfect square trinomial and simplify:**
   \[
   y = 3\left((x + 1)^2 - 1\right) + 5
   \]
   \[
   y = 3(x + 1)^2 - 3 + 5
   \]

5. **Combine like terms outside the parentheses:**
   \[
   y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2
   \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2}

First, I'll identify the general form of a quadratic equation for a parabola, which is y = ax² + bx + c. Since there are three unknown coefficients (a, b, and c), I need exactly three points to determine the equation uniquely.

Given the points (0, 4), (-1, 3), and (2, 8), I'll substitute each into the general form to create a system of equations:

1. For (0, 4):
   y = ax² + bx + c
   4 = a(0)² + b(0) + c → c = 4

2. For (-1, 3):
   y = ax² + bx + c
   3 = a(-1)² + b(-1) + 4
   Simplifying: 3 = a - b + 4 → a - b = -1

3. For (2, 8):
   y = ax² + bx + c
   8 = a(2)² + b(2) + 4
   Simplifying: 8 = 4a + 2b + 4 → 4a + 2b = 4

Now, I'll solve the system of equations derived from steps 2 and 3:

From step 2:
a - b = -1 → a = b - 1

Substitute a into equation from step 3:
4(b - 1) + 2b = 4
4b - 4 + 2b = 4 → 6b = 8 → b = 8/6 = 4/3

Now, find a using the value of b:
a = (4/3) - 1 = 1/3

Finally, with all coefficients determined:
a = 1/3, b = 4/3, c = 4
</think>

To determine the equation of the parabola that passes through the points \((2, 8)\), \((-1, 3)\), and \((0, 4)\), we'll use the general form of a quadratic equation:

\[
y = ax^2 + bx + c
\]

### Step 1: Set Up Equations Using Given Points

We substitute each point into the general form to create a system of equations.

1. **For \((0, 4)\):**
   \[
   4 = a(0)^2 + b(0) + c \implies c = 4
   \]

2. **For \((-1, 3)\):**
   \[
   3 = a(-1)^2 + b(-1) + c \implies 3 = a - b + 4 \implies a - b = -1 \quad (1)
   \]

3. **For \((2, 8)\):**
   \[
   8 = a(2)^2 + b(2) + c \implies 8 = 4a + 2b + 4 \implies 4a + 2b = 4 \quad (2)
   \]

### Step 2: Solve the System of Equations

From equation (1):
\[
a - b = -1 \implies a = b - 1
\]

Substitute \(a\) into equation (2):
\[
4(b - 1) + 2b = 4 \\
4b - 4 + 2b = 4 \\
6b = 8 \\
b = \frac{4}{3}
\]

Now, find \(a\):
\[
a = b - 1 = \frac{4}{3} - 1 = \frac{1}{3}
\]

### Step 3: Write the Final Equation

Substitute \(a\), \(b\), and \(c\) back into the general form:
\[
y = \frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4
\]

### Final Answer

### Step 1: Identify the Standard Form
For a parabola that opens horizontally (left or right), the standard form is:
\[

To find the equation of the circle, I need to determine its center and radius.

First, let's identify the endpoints of the diameter: (-2, 1) and (6, -3).

Next, I'll calculate the midpoint between these two points, which will serve as the center of the circle. To find the midpoint:
- For the x-coordinate: \(\frac{-2 + 6}{2} = 2\)
- For the y-coordinate: \(\frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = -1\)

So, the center of the circle is at (2, -1).

Now, to find the radius, I'll calculate the distance between the two endpoints and then divide by 2.

First, compute the differences in coordinates:
- \(Δx = 6 - (-2) = 8\)
- \(Δy = -3 - 1 = -4\)

Then, use the distance formula to find the diameter:
\( \text{Diameter} = \sqrt{(Δx)^2 + (Δy)^2} = \sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80} \)

Simplify \( \sqrt{80} \) to get the diameter:
\( \sqrt{80} = 4\sqrt{5} \)

Finally, divide by 2 to find the radius:
\( r = \frac{4\sqrt{5}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5} \)

With the center and radius known, I can now write the equation of the circle.

To determine the equation of the circle with endpoints of its diameter at (-2, 1) and (6, -3), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Center
The center of the circle is the midpoint between the two endpoints. The midpoint \((h, k)\) can be found using the midpoint formula:
\[
h = \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \quad k = \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2}
\]
Substituting the given points:
\[
h = \frac{-2 + 6}{2} = 2, \quad k = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = -1
\]
So, the center of the circle is at (2, -1).

### Step 2: Find the Radius
The radius is half the length of the diameter. To find the length of the diameter, use the distance formula between the two endpoints:
\[
\text{Distance} = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]
Substituting the given points:
\[
\text{Diameter} = \sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2} = \sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80} = 4\sqrt{5}
\]
Therefore, the radius \(r\) is:
\[
r = \frac{\text{Diameter}}{2} = \frac{4\sqrt{5}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5}
\]

### Step 3: Write the Equation of the Circle
The standard form of the equation of a circle with center \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\) is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
Substituting the values we found:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = (2\sqrt{5})^2
\]
Simplifying further:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 20
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the circle is:
\[
\boxed{(x-2)^2 + (y+1)^2 = 20}
\]

x^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0
\]

To convert this into the standard form, we complete the square for both \( x \) and \( y \) terms.

**Step 1: Rearrange the equation**

Group the \( x \) terms and \( y \) terms together:

\[
x^2 + 6x + y^2 - 4y = 12
\]

**Step 2: Complete the square for \( x \) terms**

The coefficient of \( x \) is 6.

- Half of 6 is 3.
- Squaring it gives \( 3^2 = 9 \).

Add and subtract this square on both sides:

\[

\]

Simplify:

\[
(x + 3)^2 + y^2 - 4y = 21
\]

- Squaring it gives \( (-2)^2 = 4 \).

Add and subtract this square on both sides:

\[

\]

Simplify:

\[
(x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
\]

- \( h = -3 \)
- \( k = 2 \)
- \( r^2 = 25 \) ⇒ \( r = 5 \)

To determine the equation of the circle with center \((-2, 5)\) passing through the point \((1, 9)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Start with the Standard Equation of a Circle
The standard form of a circle's equation is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
where:
- \((h, k)\) is the center of the circle.
- \(r\) is the radius.

### Step 2: Plug in the Given Center Coordinates
Given that the center \((-2, 5)\) corresponds to \(h = -2\) and \(k = 5\), substitute these values into the equation:
\[
(x - (-2))^2 + (y - 5)^2 = r^2 \\
\implies (x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = r^2
\]

### Step 3: Use the Given Point to Find \(r^2\)
The circle passes through the point \((1, 9)\). Substitute \(x = 1\) and \(y = 9\) into the equation:
\[
(1 + 2)^2 + (9 - 5)^2 = r^2 \\
3^2 + 4^2 = r^2 \\
9 + 16 = r^2 \\
r^2 = 25
\]

### Step 4: Determine the Radius \(r\)
Take the square root of both sides to find the radius:
\[
r = \sqrt{25} \\
r = 5
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the circle is:
\[
(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25
\]
with a radius of \(5\).

To find the equation of a circle passing through three points, I can use the general form of the circle's equation and set up a system of equations based on the given points.

First, let's assume the equation of the circle is:
\[
x^2 + y^2 + 2gx + 2fy + c = 0
\]

Now, substitute each point into this equation to create three equations:

1. For point (1,1):
\[
1 + 1 + 2g(1) + 2f(1) + c = 0 \implies 2 + 2g + 2f + c = 0
\]

2. For point (2,-3):
\[
4 + 9 + 2g(2) + 2f(-3) + c = 0 \implies 13 + 4g - 6f + c = 0
\]

3. For point (5,-1):
\[
25 + 1 + 2g(5) + 2f(-1) + c = 0 \implies 26 + 10g - 2f + c = 0
\]

\[

13 + 4g - 6f + c = 0 \\

To determine the equation of a circle passing through the points (1, 1), (2, -3), and (5, -1), follow these steps:

1. **General Form of the Circle's Equation:**
   The general form is:
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 + 2gx + 2fy + c = 0
   \]
   
2. **Substitute Each Point into the Equation:**

   - **For point (1, 1):**
     \[
     1^2 + 1^2 + 2g(1) + 2f(1) + c = 0 \\
     1 + 1 + 2g + 2f + c = 0 \\
     2 + 2g + 2f + c = 0 \quad \text{(Equation 1)}
     \]

   - **For point (2, -3):**
     \[
     2^2 + (-3)^2 + 2g(2) + 2f(-3) + c = 0 \\
     4 + 9 + 4g - 6f + c = 0 \\
     13 + 4g - 6f + c = 0 \quad \text{(Equation 2)}
     \]

   - **For point (5, -1):**
     \[
     5^2 + (-1)^2 + 2g(5) + 2f(-1) + c = 0 \\
     25 + 1 + 10g - 2f + c = 0 \\
     26 + 10g - 2f + c = 0 \quad \text{(Equation 3)}
     \]

3. **Solve the System of Equations:**

   Now, we have:
   \[
   \begin{cases}
   2 + 2g + 2f + c = 0 \\
   13 + 4g - 6f + c = 0 \\
   26 + 10g - 2f + c = 0
   \end{cases}
   \]
   
   Subtract Equation 1 from Equation 2:
   \[
   (13 - 2) + (4g - 2g) + (-6f - 2f) + (c - c) = 0 \\
   11 + 2g - 8f = 0 \quad \text{(Equation 4)}
   \]
   
   Subtract Equation 1 from Equation 3:
   \[
   (26 - 2) + (10g - 2g) + (-2f - 2f) + (c - c) = 0 \\
   24 + 8g - 4f = 0 \quad \text{(Equation 5)}
   \]
   
   Now, we have:
   \[
   \begin{cases}
   11 + 2g - 8f = 0 \\
   24 + 8g - 4f = 0
   \end{cases}
   \]
   
   Multiply Equation 4 by 4 to align coefficients of \( f \):
   \[
   44 + 8g - 32f = 0 \quad \text{(Equation 6)}
   \]
   
   Subtract Equation 5 from Equation 6:
   \[
   (44 - 24) + (8g - 8g) + (-32f + 4f) = 0 \\
   20 - 28f = 0 \\
   f = \frac{20}{28} = \frac{5}{7}
   \]
   
   Substitute \( f = \frac{5}{7} \) into Equation 4:
   \[
   11 + 2g - 8\left(\frac{5}{7}\right) = 0 \\
   11 + 2g - \frac{40}{7} = 0 \\
   2g = \frac{40}{7} - 11 \\
   2g = \frac{40}{7} - \frac{77}{7} \\
   2g = -\frac{37}{7} \\
   g = -\frac{37}{14}
   \]
   
   Substitute \( g = -\frac{37}{14} \) and \( f = \frac{5}{7} \) into Equation 1 to find \( c \):
   \[
   2 + 2\left(-\frac{37}{14}\right) + 2\left(\frac{5}{7}\right) + c = 0 \\
   2 - \frac{37}{7} + \frac{10}{7} + c = 0 \\
   \left(2 - \frac{27}{7}\right) + c = 0 \\
   \frac{14}{7} - \frac{27}{7} + c = 0 \\
   -\frac{13}{7} + c = 0 \\
   c = \frac{13}{7}
   \]
   
   So, \( g = -\frac{37}{14} \), \( f = \frac{5}{7} \), and \( c = \frac{13}{7} \).

4. **Write the Final Equation of the Circle:**
   Substitute these values back into the general equation:
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 + 2\left(-\frac{37}{14}\right)x + 2\left(\frac{5}{7}\right)y + \frac{13}{7} = 0
   \]
   
   Simplify the equation:
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 - \frac{37}{7}x + \frac{10}{7}y + \frac{13}{7} = 0
   \]
   
   To make it cleaner, multiply every term by 7 to eliminate the denominators:
   \[
   7x^2 + 7y^2 - 37x + 10y + 13 = 0
   \]

To find the center \((h, k)\) and the radius \(r\) of the circle given by the equation:

\[
x^2 + y^2 - 8x - 6y + 20 = 0
\]

we will complete the square for both \(x\) and \(y\) terms.

### Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Group the \(x\) and \(y\) terms:**
   
   \[
   x^2 - 8x + y^2 - 6y = -20
   \]

2. **Complete the square for \(x\):**

   - Coefficient of \(x\) is \(-8\).
   - Divide by 2: \(-4\).
   - Square it: \((-4)^2 = 16\).

   Add and subtract \(16\) on the left side:

   \[
   (x^2 - 8x + 16) - 16
   \]

3. **Complete the square for \(y\):**

   - Coefficient of \(y\) is \(-6\).
   - Divide by 2: \(-3\).
   - Square it: \((-3)^2 = 9\).

   Add and subtract \(9\) on the left side:

   \[
   (y^2 - 6y + 9) - 9
   \]

4. **Combine all terms:**

   \[
   (x^2 - 8x + 16) + (y^2 - 6y + 9) = -20 + 16 + 9
   \]

5. **Simplify both sides:**

   \[
   (x - 4)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = 5
   \]

Now, the equation is in the standard form of a circle:

\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]

Where:
- \(h = 4\)
- \(k = 3\)
- \(r^2 = 5\) ⇒ \(r = \sqrt{5}\)

***********************<think>
First, I recognize that the general equation of a circle is \((x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2\), where \((h, k)\) represents the center and \(r\) is the radius.

Given the center of the circle is at point (1, 2), I can substitute these values into the general equation to get:
\[
(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = r^2
\]

Next, since the circle passes through the point (4, 6), this means that when \(x = 4\) and \(y = 6\), the equation should hold true. Substituting these coordinates into the equation gives:
\[
(4 - 1)^2 + (6 - 2)^2 = r^2
\]

Calculating each term separately:
\[
3^2 = 9 \quad \text{and} \quad 4^2 = 16
\]
Adding these results together:
\[
9 + 16 = 25
\]
So, \(r^2 = 25\), which means the radius \(r\) is 5.

Therefore, the equation of the circle with center (1, 2) and passing through the point (4, 6) is:
\[
(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 5^2
\]
</think>

To determine the equation of a circle with center \((h, k)\) that passes through a specific point, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Identify the General Equation of a Circle
The general form of a circle's equation is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
where:
- \((h, k)\) is the center of the circle.
- \(r\) is the radius.

### Step 2: Plug in the Center Coordinates
Given the center \((1, 2)\), substitute these values into the general equation:
\[
(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = r^2
\]

### Step 3: Use the Given Point to Find the Radius
Since the circle passes through the point \((4, 6)\), we can plug in \(x = 4\) and \(y = 6\) into the equation to solve for \(r\):
\[
(4 - 1)^2 + (6 - 2)^2 = r^2
\]
Simplify each term:
\[
3^2 + 4^2 = r^2 \\
9 + 16 = r^2 \\
25 = r^2
\]
Take the square root of both sides to find \(r\):
\[
r = \sqrt{25} \\
r = 5
\]

### Step 4: Write the Final Equation of the Circle
Now that we know \(r = 5\) and the center is \((1, 2)\), substitute these back into the general equation:
\[
(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 5^2 \\
(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
\]

### Final Answer
The equation of the circle is:
\[
\boxed{(x - 1)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25}
\]

I start by recognizing that the given equation is \( \frac{x^2}{22} + \frac{y^2}{16} = 1 \), which represents an ellipse centered at the origin.

Next, I identify the values of \( a^2 \) and \( b^2 \). Here, \( a^2 = 22 \) and \( b^2 = 16 \).

Since \( a^2 > b^2 \), the major axis of the ellipse is along the x-axis.

To find the distance from the center to each focus (c), I use the relationship \( c = \sqrt{a^2 - b^2} \). Substituting the known values, I calculate:
\[
c = \sqrt{22 - 16} = \sqrt{6}
\]

Therefore, the foci are located at \( (\pm \sqrt{6}, 0) \).

To determine the **foci** of the ellipse given by the equation:

\[
\frac{x^2}{22} + \frac{y^2}{16} = 1
\]

follow these steps:

---

### Step 1: Identify \( a^2 \) and \( b^2 \)

The standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Here, comparing with the given equation:
- \( a^2 = 22 \)
- \( b^2 = 16 \)

---

### Step 2: Determine the Major Axis

Since \( a^2 > b^2 \), the major axis of the ellipse is **along the x-axis**.

---

### Step 3: Calculate the Distance to the Foci (c)

The distance from the center to each focus (\( c \)) is given by:

\[
c = \sqrt{a^2 - b^2}
\]

Substituting the known values:
\[
c = \sqrt{22 - 16} = \sqrt{6}
\]

---

### Step 4: Locate the Foci

Since the major axis is along the x-axis, the foci are located at:

\[
(\pm c, 0) = (\pm \sqrt{6}, 0)
\]

---

### Final Answer

The **foci** of the ellipse \( \frac{x^2}{22} + \frac{y^2}{16} = 1 \) are located at:

\[
\boxed{\left( \pm \sqrt{6}, 0 \right)}
\]

Given that we need to find the equation of an ellipse with a major axis length of 12, a minor axis length of 8, and passing through the point (3, -2), we start by noting that the semi-major axis \(a\) is 6 and the semi-minor axis \(b\) is 4. 

We consider the possibility that the ellipse could be aligned with the coordinate system. If the major axis is along the x-axis, the standard form of the ellipse would be:

\[
\frac{(x - h)^2}{36} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{16} = 1
\]

If the major axis is along the y-axis, the standard form would be:

\[
\frac{(x - h)^2}{16} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{36} = 1
\]

Given that the point (3, -2) lies on the ellipse, we need to determine if this point corresponds to a vertex or co-vertex. However, without additional information about the center or orientation, it is challenging to uniquely determine the equation.

After considering various possibilities and constraints, we conclude that the standard form of the ellipse with the given conditions is:

\[
\frac{(x - 3)^2}{16} + \frac{(y + 2)^2}{4} = 1
\]

Thus, the final answer is:

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{(x - 3)^2}{16} + \dfrac{(y + 2)^2}{4} = 1}
\]

- **Length of Major Axis**: Given as 100. Since the vertices are at (40, 0) and (-40, 0), the distance between them is \(2a\). Thus:
  
  \[
  2a = 100 \\
  a = 50
  \]
  
### Step 2: Determine the Standard Form of the Ellipse
The standard equation for an ellipse with a horizontal major axis is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Substituting \( a = 50 \):

\[

\]

***********************<think>
To find the area of an ellipse given by the equation \( \frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \), I start by identifying the standard form of an ellipse, which is \( \frac{(x-h)^2}{a^2} + \frac{(y-k)^2}{b^2} = 1 \). 

Comparing this with the given equation, I see that it's centered at the origin (h=0 and k=0), so the center remains at (0, 0).

Next, I determine the lengths of the semi-major axis (\(a\)) and the semi-minor axis (\(b\)). From the denominators under \(x^2\) and \(y^2\), it's clear that \(a^2 = 4\) and \(b^2 = 9\). Taking the square roots, I find \(a = 2\) and \(b = 3\).

With both semi-axes known, I use the formula for the area of an ellipse, which is \( \pi \times a \times b \). Substituting the values, the area becomes \( \pi \times 2 \times 3 = 6\pi \).

Finally, I present the calculated area as the final answer.
</think>

To find the area of the ellipse given by the equation:

\[
\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Identify the Standard Form of an Ellipse:**
   
   The standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin (0, 0) is:
   
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   
   Where:
   - \( a \) is the length of the semi-major axis.
   - \( b \) is the length of the semi-minor axis.

2. **Compare with the Given Equation:**
   
   By comparing the given equation with the standard form, we can identify:
   
   \[
   a^2 = 4 \quad \text{and} \quad b^2 = 9
   \]
   
3. **Calculate \( a \) and \( b \):**
   
   Taking square roots of both sides:
   
   \[
   a = \sqrt{4} = 2 \\
   b = \sqrt{9} = 3
   \]
   
4. **Use the Area Formula for an Ellipse:**
   
   The area \( A \) of an ellipse is given by:
   
   \[
   A = \pi \times a \times b
   \]
   
5. **Substitute the Values of \( a \) and \( b \):**
   
   \[
   A = \pi \times 2 \times 3 = 6\pi
   \]
   
**Final Answer:**

\[

Now, I need to determine whether it's a horizontal or vertical major axis. The denominators under the x and y terms are 9 and 16, respectively. Since 16 is larger than 9, that means the major axis is along the y-axis because the denominator under y is larger. So, this is a vertical ellipse.

In a vertical ellipse, 'a' corresponds to the semi-major axis length, so \(a^2 = 16\), which makes \(a = 4\). Then, 'b' would be with the smaller denominator, so \(b^2 = 9\) and \(b = 3\).

The distance from the center to each focus is given by c, where \(c^2 = a^2 - b^2\). Plugging in the values: \(c^2 = 16 - 9 = 7\), so \(c = \sqrt{7}\).

The center is at (1, -2). Moving up by \(\sqrt{7}\) gives one focus at \((1, -2 + \sqrt{7})\) and moving down by \(\sqrt{7}\) gives the other focus at \((1, -2 - \sqrt{7})\).

So, the foci are located at (1, -2 ± √7), which means both vertically above and below the center.

The given ellipse equation is \(\frac{(x - 1)^2}{9} + \frac{(y + 2)^2}{16} = 1\).

- **Center**: The center of the ellipse is at (1, -2).
- **Major Axis**: Since 16 > 9, the major axis is vertical.
- **Semi-Major Axis (a)**: \( a^2 = 16 \) ⇒ \( a = 4 \)
- **Semi-Minor Axis (b)**: \( b^2 = 9 \) ⇒ \( b = 3 \)
- **Distance to Foci (c)**: \( c^2 = a^2 - b^2 = 16 - 9 = 7 \) ⇒ \( c = \sqrt{7} \)

**Foci**: Located at a distance of \(\sqrt{7}\) from the center along the major axis.

Thus, the foci are at:
- \( (1, -2 + \sqrt{7}) \)
- \( (1, -2 - \sqrt{7}) \)

Given foci at (±4,0), it is determined that the hyperbola is horizontal and centered at the origin. The standard form of a horizontal hyperbola is:

\[ \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1 \]

where \( c = 4 \) (the distance from the center to each focus). For a hyperbola, the relationship between \( a \), \( b \), and \( c \) is given by:

\[ c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \]

Given \( c = 4 \), we have:

\[ c^2 = 16 = a^2 + b^2 \]

Thus, the standard form of the hyperbola can be written as:

\[ \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1 \]
where \( a^2 + b^2 = 16 \).

The equations of the asymptotes for a horizontal hyperbola are given by:

\[ y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x \]

Since we cannot determine specific numerical values for \( a \) and \( b \) with only the foci given, the general form is provided. If additional information such as the value of \( a \) or a specific point on the hyperbola were available, it would be possible to find exact equations.

### Final Answer
The standard equation of the hyperbola is:

\[ \boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{a^2} - \dfrac{y^2}{16 - a^2} = 1} \]

Alright, I'm faced with solving the equation of a hyperbola given by \( \frac{y^2}{22} - \frac{x^2}{16} = 1 \). My goal is to find its eccentricity. 

First, I need to recall the standard form of a hyperbola centered at the origin. For hyperbolas that open upwards and downwards, the equation is \( \frac{y^2}{a^2} - \frac{x^2}{b^2} = 1 \), where 'a' and 'b' are positive real numbers.

Comparing this with the given equation:
- The term under \( y^2 \) is 22, so \( a^2 = 22 \). Therefore, \( a = \sqrt{22} \).
- The term under \( x^2 \) is 16, so \( b^2 = 16 \). Thus, \( b = \sqrt{16} = 4 \).

Next, I need to find the eccentricity 'e' of the hyperbola. The formula for eccentricity in a hyperbola is:
\[ e = \sqrt{1 + \frac{b^2}{a^2}} \]

Plugging in the values of \( a^2 \) and \( b^2 \):
- \( b^2 / a^2 = 16 / 22 = 8/11 \)
- Therefore, \( e = \sqrt{1 + 8/11} = \sqrt{19/11} \)

Simplifying this:
\[ e = \frac{\sqrt{19}}{\sqrt{11}} \]
However, it's more standard to rationalize the denominator:
\[ e = \frac{\sqrt{209}}{11} \]

So, the eccentricity of the hyperbola is \( \frac{\sqrt{209}}{11} \).

   - Plug in x = 2 and y = 3 into the standard form:
     \[
     \frac{3^2}{a^2} - \frac{2^2}{b^2} = 1 \\
     \Rightarrow \frac{9}{a^2} - \frac{4}{b^2} = 1
     \]
   
4. **Set up the system of equations:**
   - From step 2:
     \[
     (\sqrt{10})^2 = a^2 + b^2 \\
     10 = a^2 + b^2
     \]
   - From step 3:
     \[
     \frac{9}{a^2} - \frac{4}{b^2} = 1
     \]
   
5. **Solve the system of equations:**
   - Let \( A = a^2 \) and \( B = b^2 \), so we have:
     \[
     A + B = 10 \\
     \frac{9}{A} - \frac{4}{B} = 1
     \]
   
6. **Express \( B \) in terms of \( A \):**
   - From the first equation: \( B = 10 - A \)
   
7. **Substitute into the second equation:**
   \[
   \frac{9}{A} - \frac{4}{10 - A} = 1
   \]
   
8. **Multiply through by \( A(10 - A) \) to eliminate denominators:**
   \[
   9(10 - A) - 4A = A(10 - A)
   \]
   \[
   90 - 9A - 4A = 10A - A^2
   \]
   \[
   90 - 13A = 10A - A^2
   \]
   
9. **Rearrange the equation:**
   \[
   A^2 - 23A + 90 = 0
   \]
   
10. **Solve the quadratic for \( A \):**
    Using the quadratic formula:
    \[
    A = \frac{23 \pm \sqrt{(23)^2 - 4(1)(90)}}{2} \\
    A = \frac{23 \pm \sqrt{529 - 360}}{2} \\
    A = \frac{23 \pm \sqrt{169}}{2} \\
    A = \frac{23 \pm 13}{2}
    \]
    
    So, \( A = \frac{36}{2} = 18 \) or \( A = \frac{10}{2} = 5 \)
    
11. **Find corresponding \( B \):**
    - If \( A = 18 \), then \( B = 10 - 18 = -8 \). But \( B \) cannot be negative.
    - Therefore, the valid solution is:
      \( A = 5 \) and \( B = 10 - 5 = 5 \)
      
12. **Write the equation of the hyperbola:**
    Substitute back into the standard form:
    \[
    \frac{y^2}{5} - \frac{x^2}{5} = 1
    \]
    
    Simplify if needed, but since both denominators are equal, it can be written as:
    \[
    y^2 - x^2 = 5
    \]

**Final Answer:**

\boxed{\dfrac{y^2}{5} - \dfrac{x^2}{5} = 1}

Given the problem of finding a hyperbola with asymptotes \( y = 2x \) and \( y = -2x \) and an eccentricity of \( \sqrt{2} \), we start by analyzing the properties of hyperbolas.

1. **Slopes of Asymptotes**: The given asymptotes are \( y = 2x \) and \( y = -2x \), which have slopes \( \pm 2 \). This suggests that the hyperbola is centered at the origin because if it were not, the asymptotes would also include a shift in their intercepts.

2. **Eccentricity**: The eccentricity of a hyperbola is given by \( e = \frac{c}{a} \), where \( c \) is the distance from the center to each focus and \( a \) is the distance from the center to each vertex. For an eccentricity of \( \sqrt{2} \), we have:
   \[
   e = \sqrt{2}
   \]

3. **Standard Form of Hyperbola**: The standard form of a hyperbola centered at the origin with horizontal transverse axis is:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   The asymptotes for this hyperbola are \( y = \pm \frac{b}{a} x \).

4. **Relating Slopes and Eccentricity**:
   - From the asymptotes, we have \( \frac{b}{a} = 2 \), so \( b = 2a \).
   - The relationship between \( a \), \( b \), and \( c \) for hyperbolas is given by \( c^2 = a^2 + b^2 \). Substituting \( b = 2a \):
     \[
     c^2 = a^2 + (2a)^2 = a^2 + 4a^2 = 5a^2
     \]
   - Therefore, \( c = a\sqrt{5} \).
   - The eccentricity is:
     \[
     e = \frac{c}{a} = \frac{a\sqrt{5}}{a} = \sqrt{5}
     \]

5. **Conclusion**: We find that the eccentricity calculated from the given asymptotes and center at the origin results in \( e = \sqrt{5} \). However, the problem specifies an eccentricity of \( \sqrt{2} \), which is not possible for a hyperbola with these asymptotes.

Thus, we conclude that there is no such hyperbola that satisfies both the given asymptotes and the specified eccentricity. Therefore, the answer is:

\[
\boxed{\text{No such hyperbola exists}}
\]

   The equation of a parabola opening upwards with vertex at the origin is:
   \[
   y = \frac{1}{4p}x^2
   \]
   where \( p \) is the distance from the vertex to the focus.

Alright, let's try to figure out how to find the equation of a parabola given its focus and directrix.

First, I remember that a parabola is defined as the set of all points that are equidistant from a fixed point called the focus and a fixed line called the directrix.

- Focus: (2, 3)
- Directrix: x = -1

To find the equation of the parabola, I can use the distance formula. The distance from any point (x, y) on the parabola to the focus must be equal to its distance to the directrix.

Let's set up the equations:

Distance from (x, y) to the focus (2, 3):
√[(x - 2)² + (y - 3)²]

Distance from (x, y) to the directrix x = -1:
|x - (-1)| = |x + 1|

Since these distances are equal:
√[(x - 2)² + (y - 3)²] = |x + 1|

To eliminate the square root, I'll square both sides:

(x - 2)² + (y - 3)² = (x + 1)²

Now, let's expand and simplify this equation.

Expand both sides:
(x² - 4x + 4) + (y² - 6y + 9) = x² + 2x + 1

Combine like terms:
x² - 4x + 4 + y² - 6y + 9 = x² + 2x + 1

Subtract x² from both sides:
-4x + 4 + y² - 6y + 9 = 2x + 1

Combine like terms:
-4x + y² - 6y + 13 = 2x + 1

Bring all terms to one side:
-4x - 2x + y² - 6y + 13 - 1 = 0
-6x + y² - 6y + 12 = 0

I can rearrange this equation for clarity:
y² - 6y - 6x + 12 = 0

This is the equation of the parabola in standard form.
</think>

To find the equation of a parabola given its focus at (2, 3) and directrix x = -1, we use the definition that any point on the parabola is equidistant from the focus and the directrix.

1. **Set up the distance equations:**
   - Distance to the focus: √[(x - 2)² + (y - 3)²]
   - Distance to the directrix: |x + 1|

2. **Equate the distances and square both sides:**
   \[
   \sqrt{(x - 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2} = |x + 1|
   \]
   Squaring both sides:
   \[
   (x - 2)^2 + (y - 3)^2 = (x + 1)^2
   \]

3. **Expand and simplify:**
   \[
   x^2 - 4x + 4 + y^2 - 6y + 9 = x^2 + 2x + 1
   \]
   Combine like terms:
   \[
   -4x + y^2 - 6y + 13 = 2x + 1
   \]

4. **Rearrange the equation:**
   \[
   y^2 - 6y - 6x + 12 = 0
   \]

The final equation of the parabola is:
\[
\boxed{y^2 - 6y - 6x + 12 = 0}
\]

To convert the given quadratic equation into **vertex form**, we'll follow these steps:

**Given Equation:**
\[ x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \]

**Step 1: Isolate the \( y \)-term**

\[
x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \\
\Rightarrow 8y = -x^2 + 4x - 12
\]

**Step 2: Divide by 8 to Solve for \( y \)**

\[
y = \frac{-x^2 + 4x - 12}{8} \\
\Rightarrow y = -\frac{1}{8}x^2 + \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{3}{2}
\]

**Step 3: Rewrite in Vertex Form**

The vertex form of a parabola is:

\[
y = a(x - h)^2 + k
\]

Where:
- \( (h, k) \) is the vertex.
- \( a \) determines if the parabola opens up or down.

We can complete the square to rewrite the equation in vertex form.

**Completing the Square:**

Start with:

\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}x^2 + \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{3}{2}
\]

Factor out \( a \) from the first two terms:

\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}\left(x^2 - 4x\right) - \frac{3}{2}
\]

Now, complete the square inside the parentheses.

Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( -2 \), and square it to get \( 4 \).

Add and subtract this value inside the parentheses:

\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}\left(x^2 - 4x + 4 - 4\right) - \frac{3}{2} \\
\Rightarrow y = -\frac{1}{8}\left((x - 2)^2 - 4\right) - \frac{3}{2}
\]

Distribute \( a \):

\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 + \frac{1}{2} - \frac{3}{2}
\]

Combine the constants:

\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1
\]

**Final Vertex Form:**

\[
y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1
\]

This is already in vertex form, where the vertex \( (h, k) \) is at \( (2, -1) \).

But maybe another way: Let me denote that the equation of any tangent to this parabola can be written in terms of its slope 'm'.

Since for a general parabola y = a(x - h)^2 +k, which we have here is k=3+1/(4a), so equation is:

y= a(x-h)^2 + 3 +1/(4a).

The tangent lines to this parabola can be written as y = m x + c. The condition that the line is tangent to the parabola is that substituting into the parabola equation gives a quadratic with discriminant zero.

So, substitute y=mx +c into the parabola:

m x + c = a(x - h)^2 + 3 +1/(4a).

Rearranged as:

a(x -h)^2 - m x + (3 +1/(4a) - c)=0

Expanding (x-h)^2: x² - 2hx + h².

So,

a(x² - 2h x + h²) -m x + (3 +1/(4a)-c)=0
=> a x² - 2a h x +a h^2 - m x + 3 +1/(4a) - c=0

Combine like terms:

a x² + (-2a h - m)x + [a h² + 3 +1/(4a) -c] = 0.

For this to be a tangent, the discriminant must be zero. The discriminant D is given by:

D = [(-2a h -m)]^2 -4 *a* (a h^2 +3 +1/(4a)-c).

Setting D=0,

[ (-2a h - m)^2 ] - 4 a (a h² +3 +1/(4a) - c ) =0

Simplify the first term:

(-2a h -m)^2 = [ -(2a h + m) ]^2 = (2a h + m)^2=4 a² h² +4 a h m + m².

Second term: 4a*(a h² +3 +1/(4a)-c )=4a* a h² +4a *3 +4a*(1/(4a)) -4a*c
=4a² h² +12a +1 -4a c

So, putting together,

(4a² h² +4a h m + m²) - (4a² h^2 +12a +1 -4a c)=0

Simplify:

4a² h² +4a h m +m² -4a² h² -12a -1 +4a c=0

Cancelling 4a² h²,

+4a h m +m² -12a -1 +4a c =0.

So, equation becomes:

m² +4 a h m +4a c -12a -1=0.

But we know that the tangent passes through (0,3), so when x=0, y=3. So for the line y=m x +c, substituting x=0 gives y=c=3.

Therefore, c=3. Substitute back into above equation:

m² +4a h m +4a*3 -12a -1 = 0

So, solving this quadratic equation for t will give us the required slopes.

1. Find equation for t: quadratic in form as above.
2. Solve for t using quadratic formula.
3. For each root t, compute d =k -t*h
4. Equations of the two tangents are y= t x +d.

To find the equations of the tangent lines from an external point \((h, k)\) to the parabola \(y = ax^2 + bx + c\), we follow these steps:

1. **Identify the general form and discriminant**:
   The equation of the parabola is given by \(y = ax^2 + bx + c\). The slope of a tangent line at any point on this parabola can be found using calculus or completing the square, but we use algebraic methods here.

2. **Formulate the tangent line**:
   Assume the equation of the tangent line is \(y = mx + n\). For it to be a tangent to the parabola, it must intersect the parabola at exactly one point. Substituting \(y = mx + n\) into the parabola's equation gives:
   \[
   ax^2 + (b - m)x + (c - n) = 0
   \]
   The discriminant of this quadratic equation must be zero for it to have exactly one solution.

3. **Calculate the discriminant**:
   The discriminant is given by:
   \[
   (b - m)^2 - 4a(c - n) = 0
   \]

4. **Express \(n\) in terms of \(m\)**:
   From the condition, we have:
   \[
   n = c + \frac{(b - m)^2}{4a}
   \]

5. **Substitute \(k\) into the equation**:
   Since the point \((h, k)\) lies on the tangent line, it must satisfy:
   \[
   k = mh + n
   \]
   Substituting \(n\) from above gives:
   \[
   k = mh + c + \frac{(b - m)^2}{4a}
   \]

6. **Solve for \(m\)**:
   Rearrange and solve the resulting quadratic equation in \(m\):
   \[
   (k - c) = mh + \frac{(b - m)^2}{4a}
   \]
   This simplifies to a quadratic equation in \(m\), which can be solved using the quadratic formula.

7. **Find the roots**:
   The solutions for \(m\) are:
   \[
   m = b - 2ah \pm 2\sqrt{a(a h^2 - bh + k)}
   \]

8. **Determine \(n\) for each \(m\)**:
   Using the value of \(m\), substitute back to find \(n\):
   \[
   n = k + c - bh + 2ah^2 \mp 2h\sqrt{a(a h^2 - bh + k)}
   \]

9. **Formulate the final equations**:
   Combining the slopes and intercepts, we get the equations of the tangent lines.

### Final Answer
The equations of the tangent lines are:
\[
\boxed{y = \left( b - 2ah \pm 2\sqrt{a(a h^2 - bh + k)} \right)x + (k + c - bh + 2ah^2 \mp 2h\sqrt{a(a h^2 - bh + k)})}
\]

To determine the axis of symmetry, focus, and directrix of the parabola given by the equation \( y = (x - 1)^2 + 5 \), we can follow these steps:

### 1. **Identify the Vertex**
The given equation is in the standard form of a parabola:
\[ y = a(x - h)^2 + k \]
where:
- \( a = 1 \)
- \( h = 1 \)
- \( k = 5 \)

So, the vertex of the parabola is at:
\[ (h, k) = (1, 5) \]

### 2. **Determine the Axis of Symmetry**
The axis of symmetry for a parabola in this form is a vertical line that passes through the vertex. Therefore, the equation of the axis of symmetry is:
\[ x = h = 1 \]

### 3. **Find the Focus**
The focus of a parabola \( y = a(x - h)^2 + k \) is located at:
\[ (h, k + \frac{1}{4a}) \]
Substituting the known values:
- \( h = 1 \)
- \( k = 5 \)
- \( a = 1 \)

So, the focus is at:
\[ \left(1, 5 + \frac{1}{4 \times 1}\right) = \left(1, 5 + \frac{1}{4}\right) = \left(1, \frac{21}{4}\right) \]

### 4. **Determine the Directrix**
The directrix of a parabola is a horizontal line located at:
\[ y = k - \frac{1}{4a} \]
Substituting the known values:
- \( k = 5 \)
- \( a = 1 \)

So, the equation of the directrix is:
\[ y = 5 - \frac{1}{4 \times 1} = 5 - \frac{1}{4} = \frac{20}{4} - \frac{1}{4} = \frac{19}{4} \]

### **Final Answer**
- Axis of Symmetry: \( x = 1 \)
- Focus: \( (1, \frac{21}{4}) \)
- Directrix: \( y = \frac{19}{4} \)

To find the equation of the parabola that passes through the points \((2, 8)\), \((-1, 3)\), and \((0, 4)\), we can use the general form of a quadratic equation:

\[
y = ax^2 + bx + c
\]

### Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Substitute the Point (0, 4)**
   
   When \(x = 0\), \(y = 4\):
   \[
   4 = a(0)^2 + b(0) + c \\
   \Rightarrow c = 4
   \]

2. **Set Up Equations Using the Other Points**

   - For Point (2, 8):
     \[
     8 = a(2)^2 + b(2) + 4 \\
     \Rightarrow 4a + 2b = 4 \quad \text{(Equation 1)}
     \]
   
   - For Point (-1, 3):
     \[
     3 = a(-1)^2 + b(-1) + 4 \\
     \Rightarrow a - b = -1 \quad \text{(Equation 2)}
     \]

   From Equation 2:
   \[
   a = b - 1
   \]
   
   Substitute \(a\) into Equation 1:
   \[
   4(b - 1) + 2b = 4 \\
   4b - 4 + 2b = 4 \\
   6b = 8 \\
   \Rightarrow b = \frac{4}{3}
   \]
   
   Then,
   \[
   a = \frac{4}{3} - 1 = \frac{1}{3}
   \]

### Final Equation

Substituting \(a\), \(b\), and \(c\) back into the general form:
\[
y = \frac{1}{3}x^2 + \frac{4}{3}x + 4
\]

k = (y₁ + y₂)/2

Plugging in the given points (-2, 1) and (6, -3):
h = (-2 + 6)/2 = 4/2 = 2
k = (1 + (-3))/2 = (-2)/2 = -1

Since this is the diameter, the radius will be half of that. So Radius r = √80 / 2.

To find the center \((h, k)\) and radius \(r\) of the circle defined by the equation \(x^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0\), we'll complete the square for both the \(x\) and \(y\) terms.

**Step 1: Group and Rearrange Terms**

Start by grouping the \(x\) terms, \(y\) terms, and moving constants to one side:

\[
x^2 + 6x + y^2 - 4y = 12
\]

**Step 2: Complete the Square for \(x\)**

Take the coefficient of \(x\), which is 6, divide by 2 to get 3, then square it to obtain 9. Add and subtract this value:

\[
(x^2 + 6x \color{red}{+ 9 - 9}) = (x + 3)^2 - 9
\]

**Step 3: Complete the Square for \(y\)**

Take the coefficient of \(y\), which is -4, divide by 2 to get -2, then square it to obtain 4. Add and subtract this value:

\[
(y^2 - 4y \color{red}{+ 4 - 4}) = (y - 2)^2 - 4
\]

**Step 4: Substitute Back into the Equation**

Replace the grouped terms in the original equation with their completed square forms:

\[
(x + 3)^2 - 9 + (y - 2)^2 - 4 = 12
\]

Combine constants on the right side:

\[
(x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
\]

**Step 5: Identify the Center and Radius**

From the equation \((x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25\), we can see that the center of the circle is at \((-3, 2)\) and the radius \(r\) is the square root of 25:

\[
h = -3,\quad k = 2,\quad r = 5
\]

**Final Answer**

The center of the circle is at \((-3, 2)\) and the radius is 5.

\]

To find the equation of the circle passing through the points (1, 1), (2, -3), and (5, -1), we start with the general equation of a circle:

\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]

where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

### Step 1: Substitute the Points into the General Equation

We substitute each point into the general equation to create a system of equations.

**For Point (1, 1):**
\[
(1 - h)^2 + (1 - k)^2 = r^2 \quad \text{(Equation 1)}
\]

**For Point (2, -3):**
\[
(2 - h)^2 + (-3 - k)^2 = r^2 \quad \text{(Equation 2)}
\]

**For Point (5, -1):**
\[
(5 - h)^2 + (-1 - k)^2 = r^2 \quad \text{(Equation 3)}
\]

### Step 2: Expand and Simplify the Equations

Expanding each equation:

**Equation 1 Expanded:**
\[
h^2 - 2h + 1 + k^2 - 2k + 1 = r^2
\]
Simplify:
\[
h^2 + k^2 - 2h - 2k + 2 = r^2 \quad \text{(Equation 1A)}
\]

**Equation 2 Expanded:**
\[
4 - 4h + h^2 + 9 + 6k + k^2 = r^2
\]
Simplify:
\[
h^2 + k^2 - 4h + 6k + 13 = r^2 \quad \text{(Equation 2A)}
\]

**Equation 3 Expanded:**
\[
25 - 10h + h^2 + 1 + 2k + k^2 = r^2
\]
Simplify:
\[
h^2 + k^2 - 10h + 2k + 26 = r^2 \quad \text{(Equation 3A)}
\]

### Step 3: Subtract Equations to Eliminate \(r^2\)

**Subtract Equation 1A from Equation 2A:**
\[
(h^2 + k^2 - 4h + 6k + 13) - (h^2 + k^2 - 2h - 2k + 2) = r^2 - r^2
\]
Simplify:
\[
-2h + 8k + 11 = 0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad h - 4k = -5.5 \quad \text{(Equation A)}
\]

**Subtract Equation 2A from Equation 3A:**
\[
(h^2 + k^2 - 10h + 2k + 26) - (h^2 + k^2 - 4h + 6k + 13) = r^2 - r^2
\]
Simplify:
\[
-6h - 4k + 13 = 0 \quad \Rightarrow \quad 3h + 2k = 6.5 \quad \text{(Equation B)}
\]

### Step 4: Solve the System of Equations (A and B)

**From Equation A:**
\[
h = -5.5 + 4k
\]

Substitute \( h = -5.5 + 4k \) into Equation B:
\[
3(-5.5 + 4k) + 2k = 6.5
\]
Simplify:
\[
-16.5 + 14k = 6.5 \quad \Rightarrow \quad 14k = 23 \quad \Rightarrow \quad k = \frac{23}{14}
\]

**Find \( h \):**
\[
h = -5.5 + 4\left(\frac{23}{14}\right) = \frac{670}{196} = \frac{28}{11}
\]

### Step 5: Find the Radius \( r \)

Using one of the original points, for example (1, 1):
\[
(1 - h)^2 + (1 - k)^2 = r^2
\]
Substitute \( h = \frac{670}{196} \) and \( k = \frac{23}{14} \):
\[
r = \sqrt{\left(\frac{670}{196}\right)^2 + \left(\frac{23}{14}\right)^2} = \frac{\sqrt{670}}{11}
\]

### Final Answer

The equation of the circle is:

\[
\boxed{\text{Final Answer} : \{\text{Numeric Answer}\}: \left(x - \frac{28}{11}\right)^2 + \left(y + \frac{21}{11}\right)^2 = \left(\frac{\sqrt{670}}{11}\right)^2}
\]

   \[
   (x^2 - 8x) + (y^2 - 6y) = -20
   \]

   - Take half of the coefficient of \( x \): \( -8/2 = -4 \)

   - Add and subtract this value inside the equation:

     \[

     \]

   Simplify the right side:
   \[

   \]

c^2 = a^2 - b^2
\]
We find:
\[
b^2 = a^2 - c^2 = 2500 - 900 = 1600 \\
b = 40
\]

Thus, the equation of the ellipse is:
\[
\frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{1600} = 1
\]

The equation of the ellipse that passes through the points (3, 0) and (0, 2) can be found using the standard form of an ellipse's equation. 

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify Key Points:**
   - The point (3, 0) lies on the x-axis.
   - The point (0, 2) lies on the y-axis.

2. **Determine Semi-Major and Semi-Minor Axes:**
   - Since (3, 0) is on the ellipse, this implies that when y = 0, x = ±a. Therefore, a (semi-major axis along the x-axis) = 3.
   - Similarly, since (0, 2) is on the ellipse, this implies that when x = 0, y = ±b. Therefore, b (semi-minor axis along the y-axis) = 2.

3. **Plug Values into Standard Form:**
   - The standard equation of an ellipse centered at the origin with major axis along the x-axis is:
     \[
     \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
     \]
   - Substituting a = 3 and b = 2, we get:
     \[
     \frac{x^2}{9} + \frac{y^2}{4} = 1
     \]

**Final Answer:**
The equation of the ellipse is \(\boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{9} + \dfrac{y^2}{4} = 1}\).

To convert the equation \(4x^2 - 9y^2 = 36\) into its standard hyperbola form, follow these steps:

**Step 1:** Divide both sides of the equation by 36 to normalize it:
\[
\frac{4x^2}{36} - \frac{9y^2}{36} = \frac{36}{36}
\]

Simplifying this, we get:
\[
\frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1
\]

**Step 2:** Recognize that the equation now is in the standard form of a hyperbola:
\[
\frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
where \(h\) and \(k\) are the coordinates of the center. In this case, there is no shift, so \(h = 0\) and \(k = 0\).

**Final Answer:**

\[

To study the hyperbola \( \frac{Y^2}{22} - \frac{X^2}{16} = 1 \), we can analyze its key features:

- **Transverse Axis**: Vertical (along Y-axis)
- **Conjugate Axis**: Horizontal (along X-axis)
- **Vertices**: Located at \( (0, \pm a) \) where \( a = \sqrt{22} \approx 4.690 \)
- **Foci**: Located at \( (0, \pm c) \) where \( c = \sqrt{a^2 + b^2} = \sqrt{38} \approx 6.164 \)
- **Eccentricity**: \( e = \frac{c}{a} = \sqrt{\frac{38}{22}} = \sqrt{\frac{19}{11}} \approx 1.265 \)
- **Asymptotes**: Equations are \( Y = \pm \left( \frac{a}{b} \right) X = \pm \left( \frac{\sqrt{22}}{4} \right) X \)

To determine the equation of a hyperbola with a horizontal transverse axis and asymptotes with slopes ±2, we follow these steps:

1. **Standard Form**: The standard form of a hyperbola centered at (h, k) with a horizontal transverse axis is:
   \[
   \frac{(x - h)^2}{a^2} - \frac{(y - k)^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   
2. **Asymptotes**: The slopes of the asymptotes for such a hyperbola are given by ±(b/a). Given that these slopes are ±2, we have:
   \[
   \frac{b}{a} = 2 \implies b = 2a
   \]
   
3. **Center at Origin**: Assuming the center (h, k) is at the origin (0, 0), the equation simplifies to:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
   \]
   
4. **Substitute b**: Substituting \( b = 2a \) into the equation, we get:
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{(2a)^2} = 1 \implies \frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{4a^2} = 1
   \]
   
5. **Simplify**: To simplify, we can let \( a^2 = 1 \), which gives:
   \[
   x^2 - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1
   \]

Thus, the equation of the hyperbola is:

\[
\boxed{x^2 - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1}
\]

The standard form of the parabola with a vertical axis (since the directrix is horizontal) opening upwards is:

\[
y = \frac{1}{4}x^2 - \frac{3}{2}x + \frac{21}{4}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Identify Key Components:** Focus at (3,4), directrix Y=2.

2. **Determine the Vertex:** The vertex lies midway between the focus and the directrix. Since they are vertically aligned (same x-coordinate for both), the y-coordinate of the vertex is the average of 4 and 2, which is 3. So, vertex at (3,3).

3. **Use Standard Equation Form:**
   - For a vertical parabola opening upwards with vertex (h,k) and focal length p:
     \[
     (x - h)^2 = 4p(y - k)
     \]
   - Here, h=3, k=3, and p is the distance from vertex to focus. Since the focus is at y=4 and vertex at y=3, p=1.

4. **Plug Values into Equation:**
   \[
   (x - 3)^2 = 4*1*(y - 3) → (x-3)^2 = 4(y -3)
   \]

5. **Expand the Equation to Solve for y:**
   - Expand left side:
     \[
     x^2 -6x +9 =4y -12
     \]
   - Bring all terms to one side and solve for y:
     \[
     x² -6x +21 = 4y → y= (x² -6x +21)/4
     \]

The equation of the parabola with focus (2, 3) and directrix x = -1 is derived as follows:

1. **Set up the distance equations**:
   - Distance from a point (x, y) to the focus (2, 3): √[(x − 2)² + (y − 3)²]
   - Distance from (x, y) to directrix x = -1: |x + 1|

2. **Set distances equal**:
   √[(x − 2)² + (y − 3)²] = |x + 1|

3. **Square both sides**:
   (x − 2)² + (y − 3)² = (x + 1)²

4. **Expand and simplify**:
   x² - 4x + 4 + y² - 6y + 9 = x² + 2x + 1
   -4x + y² - 6y + 13 = 2x + 1
   y² - 6y = 6x − 12

5. **Complete the square**:
   (y − 3)² = 6(x − 2)

The final equation of the parabola is:

To find the vertex form of the quadratic equation \( y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5 \), we'll complete the square.

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Start with the given equation:**
   \[
   y = 3x^2 + 6x + 5
   \]

2. **Factor out the coefficient of \( x^2 \) from the first two terms:**
   \[
   y = 3(x^2 + 2x) + 5
   \]

3. **Complete the square inside the parentheses:**
   - Take half of the coefficient of \( x \), which is \( 1 \).
   - Square this value to get \( 1 \).

4. **Add and subtract this squared value inside the equation:**
   \[
   y = 3\left( (x^2 + 2x + 1) - 1 \right) + 5
   \]

5. **Rewrite as a perfect square trinomial and simplify:**
   \[
   y = 3\left( (x + 1)^2 - 1 \right) + 5
   \]
   \[
   y = 3(x + 1)^2 - 3 + 5
   \]

6. **Combine the constant terms:**
   \[
   y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2
   \]

**Final Answer:**

The vertex form of the equation is:
\[
y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2
\]
\[
\boxed{\text{Vertex Form: } y = 3(x + 1)^2 + 2}
\]

The given equation is a quadratic in \(x\), which represents a parabola opening upwards or downwards. To convert it into vertex form, we complete the square.

**Step 1: Start with the given equation**
\[ x^2 - 4x + 8y + 12 = 0 \]

**Step 2: Move constants and \(y\) term to the other side**
\[ x^2 - 4x = -8y - 12 \]

**Step 3: Complete the square for the \(x\)-terms**
Take half of the coefficient of \(x\), which is \(-4/2 = -2\). Square it to get \((-2)^2 = 4\).

Add and subtract this value on the left side:
\[ x^2 - 4x + 4 - 4 = -8y - 12 \]

**Step 4: Simplify**
\[ (x - 2)^2 - 4 = -8y - 12 \]

Move constants to the right side:
\[ (x - 2)^2 = -8y - 8 \]

Factor out \(-8\) from the right side:
\[ (x - 2)^2 = -8(y + 1) \]

**Step 5: Express in vertex form**
Divide both sides by \(-8\):
\[ y + 1 = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 \]

Subtract 1 from both sides:
\[ y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1 \]

**Final Answer:**
The equation in vertex form is \( y = -\frac{1}{8}(x - 2)^2 - 1 \), with the vertex at (2, -1).

The equation of the parabola passing through the points (2,8), (-1,3), and (0,4) is found by solving for a, b, and c in the general form y = ax² + bx + c. Using substitution based on each point:

- From (0,4): c = 4.
- From (-1,3): -1 = a - b → a = b - 1.
- From (2,8) and substituting a: 6b - 4 = 4 → b = 4/3. Then, a = 1/3.

Thus, the equation is y = (1/3)x² + (4/3)x + 4.

Alright, let's tackle this problem step by step. The goal is to find the equation of a parabola given its vertex and focus.

**Given:**
- **Vertex:** (-4, 1)
- **Focus:** (-2, 1)

**Step 1: Determine the Direction of the Parabola**

First, we need to determine whether the parabola opens upwards, downwards, to the left, or to the right. This is based on the positions of the vertex and the focus.

Since both the vertex and the focus have the same y-coordinate (both are at y = 1), this indicates that the axis of symmetry of the parabola is **horizontal**. That means the parabola opens either to the left or to the right.

**Step 2: Find the Distance Between the Vertex and Focus**

Next, we calculate the distance between the vertex and the focus to determine how "wide" or "narrow" the parabola is.

The formula for the distance (denoted as \( p \)) from the vertex to the focus in a horizontally opening parabola is:

\[
p = |x_{\text{focus}} - x_{\text{vertex}}|
\]

Plugging in the given values:

\[
p = |-2 - (-4)| = |-2 + 4| = |2| = 2
\]

So, \( p = 2 \). This tells us that the focus is 2 units to the right of the vertex.

**Step 3: Determine the Sign of \( p \)**

Since the parabola opens to the **right**, \( p \) should be positive. If it opened to the left, \( p \) would have been negative.

So, we'll use \( p = 2 \).

**Step 4: Write the Standard Form of the Equation**

The standard form of a horizontally opening parabola with vertex at (h, k) is:

\[
(y - k)^2 = 4p(x - h)
\]

Plugging in our values:
- \( h = -4 \)
- \( k = 1 \)
- \( p = 2 \)

So,

\[
(y - 1)^2 = 4(2)(x - (-4)) \\
(y - 1)^2 = 8(x + 4)
\]

**Step 5: Simplify the Equation (if necessary)**

In this case, no further simplification is needed. The equation \( (y - 1)^2 = 8(x + 4) \) accurately represents the parabola.

**Final Answer**

The equation of the parabola with vertex (-4, 1) and focus at (-2, 1) is:

(y - 1)^2 = 8(x + 4)

I need to determine the equation of a circle given the endpoints of its diameter, which are (-2, 1) and (6, -3). 

First, I'll find the center of the circle by calculating the midpoint between these two points. The midpoint formula is ((x₁ + x₂)/2, (y₁ + y₂)/2).

Next, I'll calculate the distance between the two endpoints to find the length of the diameter using the distance formula: √[(x₂ - x₁)² + (y₂ - y₁)²]. Once I have the diameter, I can divide it by 2 to get the radius.

Finally, I'll substitute the center coordinates and the radius into the standard equation of a circle: (x - h)² + (y - k)² = r².
</think>

To determine the equation of the circle with endpoints of its diameter at \((-2, 1)\) and \((6, -3)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Find the Center
The center of the circle is the midpoint between the two endpoints. Use the midpoint formula:
\[
(h, k) = \left( \frac{x_1 + x_2}{2}, \frac{y_1 + y_2}{2} \right)
\]
Substitute the given points:
\[
h = \frac{-2 + 6}{2} = \frac{4}{2} = 2
\]
\[
k = \frac{1 + (-3)}{2} = \frac{-2}{2} = -1
\]
So, the center of the circle is \((2, -1)\).

### Step 2: Find the Radius
The radius is half the length of the diameter. First, calculate the distance between the two endpoints using the distance formula:
\[
d = \sqrt{(x_2 - x_1)^2 + (y_2 - y_1)^2}
\]
Substitute the given points:
\[
d = \sqrt{(6 - (-2))^2 + (-3 - 1)^2} = \sqrt{8^2 + (-4)^2} = \sqrt{64 + 16} = \sqrt{80} = 4\sqrt{5}
\]
Now, divide by 2 to find the radius:
\[
r = \frac{d}{2} = \frac{4\sqrt{5}}{2} = 2\sqrt{5}
\]

### Step 3: Write the Equation of the Circle
Using the standard form of a circle's equation:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
Substitute \(h = 2\), \(k = -1\), and \(r = 2\sqrt{5}\):
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y - (-1))^2 = (2\sqrt{5})^2
\]
Simplify:
\[
(x - 2)^2 + (y + 1)^2 = 4 \times 5 = 20
\]

### Final Answer

The equation of the circle is:

- Square it to get \( 9 \).

**For the \( y \)-terms:**

To find the center and radius of the circle given by the equation:

\[
x^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0
\]

**Step-by-Step Solution:**

1. **Rewrite the Equation in Standard Form**
   
   The standard form of a circle's equation is:
   \[
   (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
   \]
   where \( (h, k) \) is the center and \( r \) is the radius.

   Starting with the given equation:
   \[
   x^2 + y^2 + 6x - 4y - 12 = 0
   \]

2. **Group Like Terms**
   
   Group the \( x \)-terms and \( y \)-terms together:
   \[
   (x^2 + 6x) + (y^2 - 4y) = 12
   \]

3. **Complete the Square for \( x \)-terms**
   
   To complete the square for \( x^2 + 6x \):
   - Take half of the coefficient of \( x \): \( \frac{6}{2} = 3 \)
   - Square it: \( 3^2 = 9 \)

4. **Complete the Square for \( y \)-terms**
   
   To complete the square for \( y^2 - 4y \):
   - Take half of the coefficient of \( y \): \( \frac{-4}{2} = -2 \)
   - Square it: \( (-2)^2 = 4 \)

5. **Add the Squares to Both Sides**
   
   Add the completed squares to both sides of the equation:
   \[
   (x^2 + 6x + 9) + (y^2 - 4y + 4) = 12 + 9 + 4
   \]
   Simplifying:
   \[
   (x + 3)^2 + (y - 2)^2 = 25
   \]

6. **Identify the Center and Radius**
   
   Comparing with the standard form \( (x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2 \):
   - Center: \( (-3, 2) \)
   - Radius: \( \sqrt{25} = 5 \)

   \[

   \]

\]

To find the equation of a circle with center \((-2, 5)\) that passes through the point \((1, 9)\), follow these steps:

### Step 1: Use the General Equation of a Circle
The general equation of a circle is:
\[
(x - h)^2 + (y - k)^2 = r^2
\]
where \((h, k)\) is the center and \(r\) is the radius.

Given the center \((-2, 5)\), we substitute \(h = -2\) and \(k = 5\) into the equation:
\[
(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = r^2
\]

### Step 2: Find the Radius Using the Given Point
Since the circle passes through \((1, 9)\), substitute \(x = 1\) and \(y = 9\) into the equation to find \(r\):
\[
(1 + 2)^2 + (9 - 5)^2 = r^2 \\
3^2 + 4^2 = r^2 \\
9 + 16 = r^2 \\
25 = r^2
\]
So, \(r = \sqrt{25} = 5\).

### Step 3: Write the Complete Equation of the Circle
Substitute \(r = 5\) back into the equation:
\[
(x + 2)^2 + (y - 5)^2 = 25
\]

### Final Answer
\[
\boxed{(x+2)^2+(y-5)^2=25}

The equation of the ellipse is determined using the given center, major axis length, and minor axis length.

**Solution:**

- **Center**: The ellipse is centered at \((h, k) = (3, -2)\).
- **Major Axis Length**: Given as 12. Since it's vertical, this corresponds to \(2a\), so:
  \[
  a = \frac{12}{2} = 6
  \]
- **Minor Axis Length**: Given as 8. This corresponds to \(2b\), so:
  \[
  b = \frac{8}{2} = 4
  \]

The standard form of the ellipse's equation with a vertical major axis is:
\[
\frac{(x - h)^2}{b^2} + \frac{(y - k)^2}{a^2} = 1
\]
Substituting \(h = 3\), \(k = -2\), \(a = 6\), and \(b = 4\):
\[
\frac{(x - 3)^2}{4^2} + \frac{(y + 2)^2}{6^2} = 1
\]
Simplifying:
\[
\frac{(x - 3)^2}{16} + \frac{(y + 2)^2}{36} = 1
\]

To convert the equation \( 9x^2 + 4y^2 = 36 \) into the standard form of an ellipse, follow these steps:

1. **Divide both sides by 36** to normalize the equation:
   \[
   \frac{9x^2}{36} + \frac{4y^2}{36} = \frac{36}{36}
   \]
   
2. **Simplify the fractions:**
   \[
   \frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
   \]

Now, the equation is in the standard form of an ellipse:
\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
where \( a^2 = 4 \) and \( b^2 = 9 \).

### Final Answer
The equation \( 9x^2 + 4y^2 = 36 \) in the standard form of an ellipse is:
\[
\boxed{\frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1}

Okay, I need to find the eccentricity of an ellipse given by the equation x²/36 + y²/9 = 1. Hmm, first I remember that the general form of an ellipse is (x²/a²) + (y²/b²) = 1, where a and b are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes. But wait, sometimes it's written differently depending on which denominator is larger.

So in this case, comparing x²/36 and y²/9, I see that 36 is bigger than 9. That means the major axis is along the x-axis because the denominator under x² is larger. So a² = 36, so a would be sqrt(36) which is 6. Similarly, b² = 9, so b is sqrt(9) = 3.

Now, I recall that the formula for eccentricity (e) of an ellipse is e = c/a, where c is the distance from the center to a focus, and it's calculated as c = sqrt(a² - b²). So first, I'll compute a² minus b². 

a² is 36 and b² is 9, so subtracting them gives 36 - 9 = 27. Then c is the square root of 27. But wait, sqrt(27) can be simplified to 3*sqrt(3). So c = 3√3.

Now, with a being 6 and c being 3√3, I need to find e by dividing c by a. That would be (3√3)/6. Simplifying this fraction, both numerator and denominator are divisible by 3. So that reduces to (√3)/2.

Let me double-check: if a is 6 and b is 3, then yes, the major axis is along x with length 12 and minor along y with length 6. Eccentricity e = sqrt(1 - (b²/a²)) which would be sqrt(1 - (9/36)) = sqrt(1 - 0.25) = sqrt(0.75) = √3/2. Yep, that matches what I got earlier.

So the eccentricity is √3 divided by 2.
</think>

The given equation of the ellipse is \( \frac{x^2}{36} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \). Comparing it with the standard form \( \frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1 \), we identify \( a^2 = 36 \) and \( b^2 = 9 \). Thus, \( a = 6 \) and \( b = 3 \).

The eccentricity \( e \) of an ellipse is calculated using the formula:
\[
e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{b^2}{a^2}}
\]
Substituting the values:
\[
e = \sqrt{1 - \frac{9}{36}} = \sqrt{1 - 0.25} = \sqrt{0.75} = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
\]

The user provided the information that the major axis of an ellipse is 100 and asked for further details or confirmation. The points (-40, 0) and (40, 0) were mentioned, but it was unclear if they are endpoints of the major or minor axis.

Given the major axis length is 100, we know that the semi-major axis \(a\) is 50. The standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin with a horizontal major axis is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

where \(a = 50\). Therefore, the equation becomes:

\[
\frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Without additional information about another point or condition on the ellipse, we cannot determine the value of \(b\) (the semi-minor axis). However, since the major axis length was explicitly given as 100, the final answer is:

\[
\boxed{100}
\]

First, I identify that the ellipse has its center at (0, 0), an major axis length of 100 units, and foci located at (±30, 0).

The standard equation for an ellipse centered at the origin with a horizontal major axis is \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\), where \(a\) is the semi-major axis and \(b\) is the semi-minor axis.

Given that the length of the major axis is 100, I calculate the semi-major axis:
\( a = \frac{100}{2} = 50 \).

Next, using the relationship between the distance to the foci (\(c\)) and the ellipse parameters \(a\) and \(b\):
\( c^2 = a^2 - b^2 \)
Plugging in the known values:
\( 30^2 = 50^2 - b^2 \)
Solving for \(b^2\):
\( 900 = 2500 - b^2 \)
\( b^2 = 1600 \)

Finally, substituting these values into the standard equation of the ellipse:
\(\frac{x^2}{2500} + \frac{y^2}{1600} = 1 \)

The standard form of an ellipse with a horizontal major axis is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

**Given:**
- Center at (0, 0)
- Major axis length (\(2a\)) = 100 → \( a = 50 \)
- Foci at (\(\pm c\), 0) where \( c = 30 \)

**Using the relationship between \(a\), \(b\), and \(c\) for an ellipse:**

\[
c^2 = a^2 - b^2
\]

Plugging in the known values:

\[
30^2 = 50^2 - b^2 \\
900 = 2500 - b^2 \\
b^2 = 2500 - 900 \\
b^2 = 1600
\]

**Substituting into the standard equation:**

\[
\frac{x^2}{50^2} + \frac{y^2}{40^2} = 1
\]

Given the problem, we need to find the standard form of an ellipse's equation. The key steps are as follows:

1. **Identify the given points and their significance**:
   - Points (0, ±5) indicate foci on the y-axis.
   - Point (7, 0) lies on the x-axis.

2. **Determine the major axis**:
   - Since the foci are on the y-axis, the major axis is along the y-axis.

3. **Form of the ellipse equation**:
   - The standard form for an ellipse with a vertical major axis is \(\frac{x^2}{b^2} + \frac{y^2}{a^2} = 1\) where \(a > b\).

4. **Calculate semi-minor axis (b)**:
   - Given the point (7, 0) on the ellipse: \(\frac{7^2}{b^2} = 1 \implies b^2 = 49 \implies b = 7\).

5. **Calculate distance from center to foci (c)**:
   - Given foci at (0, ±5): \(c = 5\).

6. **Relate a and c using the relationship \(c^2 = a^2 - b^2\)**:
   - \(25 = a^2 - 49 \implies a^2 = 74\).

7. **Form the ellipse equation**:
   - Substituting \(b^2 = 49\) and \(a^2 = 74\), we get the standard form: \(\frac{x^2}{49} + \frac{y^2}{74} = 1\).

Thus, the standard form of the ellipse's equation is:

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{x^2}{49} + \dfrac{y^2}{74} = 1}
\]

The standard form of an ellipse centered at the origin is given by:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Where \( a \) and \( b \) are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes, respectively.

Given that the ellipse passes through the points (3, 0) and (0, 2), we can determine these values:

To find the area of the ellipse defined by the equation \( \frac{x^2}{4} + \frac{y^2}{9} = 1 \), we can follow these steps:

**Step 1: Identify the Standard Form of the Ellipse**

The standard form of an ellipse is:
\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} + \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
where \( a \) and \( b \) are the lengths of the semi-major and semi-minor axes, respectively.

**Step 2: Determine \( a \) and \( b \)**

Comparing the given equation with the standard form:
\[
a^2 = 4 \quad \Rightarrow \quad a = 2
\]
\[
b^2 = 9 \quad \Rightarrow \quad b = 3
\]

**Step 3: Calculate the Area of the Ellipse**

The area \( A \) of an ellipse is given by:
\[
A = \pi \cdot a \cdot b
\]
Substituting the values of \( a \) and \( b \):
\[
A = \pi \cdot 2 \cdot 3 = 6\pi
\]

The area of the ellipse is:
\[
\boxed{6\pi}

The standard form of a hyperbola centered at the origin opening horizontally is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]

Given that the foci are at \( (\pm5\sqrt{3}, 0) \), we know that \( c = 5\sqrt{3} \). The relationship between \( a \), \( b \), and \( c \) for hyperbolas is:

\[
c^2 = a^2 + b^2
\]

The slopes of the asymptotes are given by \( \pm \frac{b}{a} \). From prior calculations, it was determined that these slopes satisfy \( m^2 = \frac{3}{4} \), so:

\[
\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)^2 = \frac{3}{4} \implies b^2 = \frac{3}{4}a^2
\]

Substituting into the equation for \( c^2 \):

\[
(5\sqrt{3})^2 = a^2 + \frac{3}{4}a^2 \\
75 = \frac{7}{4}a^2 \\
a^2 = 75 \times \frac{4}{7} = \frac{300}{7}
\]

Then, \( b^2 \) is:

\[
b^2 = \frac{3}{4} \times \frac{300}{7} = \frac{900}{28} = \frac{225}{7}
\]

Thus, the equation of the hyperbola is:

\[
\frac{x^2}{\frac{300}{7}} - \frac{y^2}{\frac{225}{7}} = 1
\]

**Asymptotes:**
For such hyperbolas, the equations of the asymptotes are given by:
\[ y = \pm \frac{b}{a}x \]

- \( a = 3 \)
- \( b = 4 \)

Thus, the equations of the asymptotes are:
\[ y = \pm \frac{4}{3}x \]

**Foci:**
The foci of a hyperbola are located at a distance \( c \) from the center along the transverse axis (the x-axis in this case). The relationship between \( a \), \( b \), and \( c \) for hyperbolas is:

- \( c^2 = 9 + 16 = 25 \)
- \( c = \sqrt{25} = 5 \)

I need to convert the given equation \(4x^2 - 9y^2 = 36\) into the standard form of a hyperbola.

First, I'll divide both sides by 36 to normalize the right-hand side to 1. This gives me \(\frac{4x^2}{36} - \frac{9y^2}{36} = 1\).

Next, I'll simplify each term by reducing the coefficients in the denominators. Dividing both numerator and denominator of \(4x^2\) by 4 results in \(\frac{x^2}{9}\). Similarly, dividing \(9y^2\) by 9 gives me \(\frac{y^2}{4}\).

Now, substituting these simplified terms back into the equation, it becomes \(\frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1\), which is the standard form of a hyperbola.
</think>

To convert the given equation \(4x^2 - 9y^2 = 36\) into the **standard form** of a **hyperbola**, follow these steps:

### Step 1: Divide Both Sides by 36
The goal is to normalize the equation so that it equals 1.

\[
\frac{4x^2}{36} - \frac{9y^2}{36} = \frac{36}{36}
\]

### Step 2: Simplify Each Term
Simplify each fraction by reducing the coefficients in the denominators.

\[
\frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1
\]

### Final Answer

The equation \(4x^2 - 9y^2 = 36\) in standard form is:

\[
\boxed{\frac{x^2}{9} - \frac{y^2}{4} = 1}
\]

First, I identify that the hyperbola has vertices at (5,0) and (-5,0), which means it opens horizontally. The standard form of such a hyperbola is \(\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1\).

Next, I determine \(a\) by noting that the distance from the center to each vertex is 5 units, so \(a = 5\). This gives me \(a^2 = 25\).

Given the slopes of the asymptotes are ±3/5, I know that \(\frac{b}{a} = \frac{3}{5}\). Substituting \(a = 5\) into this equation allows me to solve for \(b\), resulting in \(b = 3\). Therefore, \(b^2 = 9\).

Finally, I substitute \(a^2\) and \(b^2\) back into the standard form of the hyperbola equation to get \(\frac{x^2}{25} - \frac{y^2}{9} = 1\).

To determine the equation of the hyperbola with vertices at \((5, 0)\) and \((-5, 0)\), and asymptotes with slopes \(±\frac{3}{5}\), follow these steps:

### **Step 1: Identify the orientation of the hyperbola**
Since the vertices are on the x-axis (\(y = 0\)), the hyperbola opens horizontally. The standard form of a horizontally opening hyperbola is:
\[
\frac{x^2}{a^2} - \frac{y^2}{b^2} = 1
\]
where \(a\) is the distance from the center to each vertex.

### **Step 2: Determine \(a\)**
The vertices are at \((5, 0)\) and \((-5, 0)\), so:
\[
a = 5
\]
Thus,
\[
a^2 = 25
\]

### **Step 3: Use the slopes of the asymptotes to find \(b\)**
The slopes of the asymptotes for a horizontally opening hyperbola are given by:
\[
\text{Slope} = \pm \frac{b}{a}
\]
Given the slopes are \(±\frac{3}{5}\), we have:
\[
\frac{b}{5} = \frac{3}{5} \implies b = 3
\]
Therefore,
\[
b^2 = 9
\]

### **Step 4: Write the equation of the hyperbola**
Substitute \(a^2\) and \(b^2\) into the standard form:
\[
\frac{x^2}{25} - \frac{y^2}{9} = 1
\]

---

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{\dfrac{x^{2}}{25} - \dfrac{y^{2}}{9} = 1}

\frac{X^2}{A^2} - \frac{Y^2}{B^2} = 1
\]

This represents a **horizontal hyperbola** because the positive term is with \( X^2 \). However, the user might be asking about properties like asymptotes or foci regardless of orientation.

### Step 2: Find the foci

For a hyperbola, the distance from the center to each focus (denoted as \( C \)) is given by:

\[
C = \sqrt{A^2 + B^2}
\]

From our equation:
- \( A^2 = 4 \) → \( A = 2 \)
- \( B^2 = 9 \) → \( B = 3 \)

So,

\[
C = \sqrt{4 + 9} = \sqrt{13} \approx 3.605
\]

The foci are located along the **horizontal axis** (since it's a horizontal hyperbola), at points:

\[
(\pm C, 0) = (\pm \sqrt{13}, 0)
\]

### Step 3: Find the vertical axis

In this context, "vertical axis" likely refers to the conjugate axis. For a hyperbola of the form \( \frac{X^2}{A^2} - \frac{Y^2}{B^2} = 1 \), the **transverse axis** (the one that passes through the foci) is horizontal, and its length is \( 2A \). The **conjugate axis** (perpendicular to the transverse axis) has a length of \( 2B \).

So, the lengths are:
- Transverse axis: \( 2 \times 2 = 4 \)
- Conjugate axis: \( 2 \times 3 = 6 \)

### Step 4: Find the asymptotes

The equations for the asymptotes of a hyperbola centered at the origin are:

\[
Y = \pm \frac{B}{A} X
\]

Substituting our values:
- \( A = 2 \)
- \( B = 3 \)

So, the equations are:

\[
Y = \pm \frac{3}{2} X
\]

### Final Answer

Putting it all together:

- **Foci**: \( (\pm \sqrt{13}, 0) \approx (\pm 3.605, 0) \)
- **Conjugate axis (vertical)**: Length is \( 6 \)
- **Asymptotes**: \( Y = \frac{3}{2} X \) and \( Y = -\frac{3}{2} X \)
